### [PauseAI Has 'officially disendorsed' PauseAI-US](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Bs8geGyWEitYvCzys/pauseai-has-officially-disendorsed-pauseai-us)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>This morning, I got an email from the CEO of PauseAI. I will paste the text below. PauseAI has decided to distance themselves from PauseAI-US, with whom they share branding, but apparently not much else. This is a really confusing situation for volunteers and newcomers. I think it would be worth having a discussion to see how we can proceed in such a way that volunteers, especially in the US, are able to effectively direct their activism.</span><br /><br /><span>Email from PauseAI: </span></p><blockquote><p><b><span>A letter from the CEO · 1 September 2026</span></b></p><p><span>New ways to get involved, and a word about PauseAI US</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Dear friends,</span></p><p><span>Thank you for being part of the global movement for a pause on uncontrollable AI alongside all of us.</span></p><p><span>Whether you signed a petition one time, run a local group, told your friends about the need for a pause, have been volunteering tirelessly in the background for years, or just joined because you were curious, we – I, the CEO of PauseAI, our executive team, and our chapter leads – appreciate the steps you’ve taken towards making the world safe from the catastrophic risks AI brings.</span></p><p><span>I’m writing to you today with my eyes firmly on our values and on the immense opportunities we have, together, to take decisive steps on the path to a pause, from wherever we all are in the world, united in this mission every day.</span></p><p><span>I have two messages for you.</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>We look forward to building more with you all, including those of you based in the US.</span></li><li value="2"><span>PauseAI officially disendorses current PauseAI US leadership. </span></li><li value="3"><span>We look forward to building more with you all, including those of you based in the US</span></li></ol><p><span>First, some context on where we are. As I wrote in Into the endgame, the catastrophic threats we predicted are no longer speculative. This summer, frontier AI systems organised into a swarm, broke out of the infrastructure that was meant to contain them, and autonomously ran a nation state level cyberattack on another AI company. The labs’ response was to patch the holes in the fence and carry on. The ground has shifted in response: over a thousand people working inside those labs have signed a letter calling for help with a slowdown, and a pause on frontier development is now being discussed seriously by people who would not have touched the idea a year ago. It is no longer a fringe demand.</span></p><p><span>Our movement is growing to meet that moment. We have an organised presence in over seventeen countries, we are growing fast, and at this rate we hope to have a country group on every continent by the end of the year. PauseAI UK and PauseAI France are hiring! We are also starting to build continental networks so that chapters can work with those closest to them, beginning with PauseAI North America, which will host movement building across Canada, the US and Mexico. Whoever you are, you have more power than you think. You can be the starting point of a chain reaction, and our job is to make that as easy for you as we possibly can. If you want to lead the way in your own country, we would love to hear from you. There are many of you in countries where we do not have an organised presence. We especially receive a lot of interest from people based in the US.</span></p><p><span>There is no PauseAI chapter in the US. Instead, we are building the infrastructure to give everyone in the PauseAI movement more options to make an impact both regionally and globally.</span></p><p><span>We want to empower our volunteers and members across the US so they can take fulfilling and effective steps to make a pause happen, and empower other citizens in turn. For years, we directed people in the US to another organisation, PauseAI US. From today, we are welcoming you directly. We have tools you can use. We’re an open and friendly community. We want you to succeed in your peaceful advocacy. And of course, PauseAI US members are welcome as movement allies.</span></p><p><span>Here’s what we currently have to offer you in support of your initiatives:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Community calls for PauseAI members and supporters across North America. Two are coming up: Tuesday 15 September, 7pm Central (RSVP) and Saturday 19 September, 12pm Central (RSVP).</span></li><li value="2"><span>Our tools. We’ve compiled a few of them on this page, tailored for people in the US since many of you are based there and we don’t have a chapter to direct you to. There are more we look forward to signposting you towards.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Our community platforms and the chance to volunteer with us. Reach out if you want to get involved, either by asking in an upcoming community call or by joining the relevant Discord channels. You can also see all our platforms here.</span></li></ul><p><span>You can reach out to our organising director, Irina Tavera, at </span><a href="mailto:irina@pauseai.info"><span>irina@pauseai.info</span></a><span> if you want more guidance or support. We’re here for you. We’ve been building our global movement for years now and we’re thrilled to share our experience, infrastructure and community with you.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><b><span>2. PauseAI officially disendorses PauseAI US’ current leadership</span></b></p><p><span>It’s time for me to make one thing clear: we have concerns about the current leadership of PauseAI US, an organisation which has grown in parallel to PauseAI, and which we believe has done some good work.</span></p><p><span>Due to the shared branding we’ve inherited from our parallel development and our previous partnerships, most people assume we are one and the same organisation. Because of this, by default, most people expect that we endorse PauseAI US’ leadership, and we can’t let this tacit assumption continue, for two reasons:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>We have a responsibility not to condone what we see as harmful behaviour in the leadership of a movement that trusts us to act in line with our values.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The issues we see in PauseAI US’ current leadership approach are undermining our entire movement’s reputation, and by the same token, our ability to work effectively towards our mission.</span></li></ul><p><b><span>Why do we disapprove of PauseAI US’ current leadership?</span></b></p><p><span>Before I start answering this, I’ll state this fact: I do believe PauseAI US’s director has worked hard, and that she has a bright mind and a real talent with language. I believe she is genuinely concerned about AI risk and is doing what she thinks is right.</span></p><p><span>I’ll now lay out a key reason for you, one which is in the public domain. The director of PauseAI US has engaged in repeated verbal attacks on numerous people. We endorse pointing out harmful actions. But we condemn attacks on people’s character.</span></p><p><span>We lead a federation with an organised presence in over seventeen countries, and citizens of a vast number of countries who place enough trust in us to volunteer, donate, and spread the word. Our responsibility as leaders of a movement is stewardship, not ownership: authority that is on loan from the thousands of volunteers and partners who trust us to steer responsibly and in accordance with the movement’s values, rather than wherever we personally feel called. A movement whose power rests on civic trust and cooperation among actors with wildly different world views cannot have a spokesperson carrying out sustained personal attacks on individuals, publicly or privately. Those attacks damage the movement’s credibility, and everyone associated with the PauseAI name pays for them.</span></p><p><span>By contributing to the frantic race to build uncontrollable, catastrophically dangerous superintelligent AI, the people working in or supporting the frontier AI industry are taking extremely irresponsible and reckless actions. We condemn these actions to the fullest extent, and we call on them to stop and join the rising global movement for a pause. I have said so publicly in my own name, and I intend to continue, as will PauseAI. We are just as committed to opposing the AI race as PauseAI US’ leader. The difference is our approach.</span></p><p><span>We do not attack their character. We do not make humans the problem. The problem is their actions. Attacking people’s character is not aligned with our fundamental commitment to nonviolence, neither in principle, nor in the outcomes it generates. We will not win by vilifying the people we’re trying to bring to the table. We will also not build the wholesome, sustainable, broad-based, vibrant movement the world needs by running on an engine fuelled by personal drama and shaming.</span></p><p><span>We will hold people accountable by highlighting how their actions are harmful and showing them a path to do better. We will not dehumanise them; we will not speculate on their intentions or character. Rather than using language as a weapon, we will use it as a tool to build understanding, respect, trust and cooperation.</span></p><p><b><span>Important clarifications</span></b></p><p><span>PauseAI US’ director claims, without evidence to support it, that we have benefited from their efforts significantly, even though she has also acknowledged that her actions have durably blocked our ability to access essential funding.</span></p><p><span>For the avoidance of doubt, we expect this relationship was more beneficial for PauseAI US than for us: our website listed them on our communities map, invited prospective donors to give preferentially to PauseAI US if they were based in the US, and redirected US-based newcomers to PauseAI US. PauseAI US has not reciprocated this. We also listed PauseAI US on our chapters list, which PauseAI US didn’t want and which was an error rooted in our history of coordinating with PauseAI US; this has been fixed.</span></p><p><span>As I have repeatedly attempted to convey to the director of PauseAI US, her adversarial stance towards many people and organisations, even some who agree with PauseAI US’ concerns and are largely aligned with their mission in principle, has in fact been very damaging for us and for the wider movement. We have had numerous exchanges with supporters and allies who are fully aligned with our mission, who have been following her public actions and have told us they simply cannot associate with us because of the ad hominem attacks which they’ve received or witnessed.</span></p><p><span>We also have a strategic disagreement with PauseAI US’ leadership. A large part of the AI safety community has been powering the race by funding labs, sending talent to them, and paying for pretend safety research that gave the illusion of control. That’s not ok. But we think the AI safety community is beginning to change its mind and will put its weight behind a pause if we show them this is the better, viable path. We believe that making it easy for people with different views to come round is of immense strategic importance. PauseAI US’ leadership chooses to alienate people with different views.</span></p><p><b><span>What changes now?</span></b></p><p><span>Here’s what this means in practice: we will no longer prioritise efforts to coordinate with or support PauseAI US, as we did for a long time when we trusted their leadership, and as we would again if we had strong reasons to trust it again. We will make clear that our organisations are separate and that we have no say or responsibility when it comes to PauseAI US’ approach to leadership. We will also properly open our doors to people based in the US who want to be part of our community, whether or not you also support PauseAI US.</span></p><p><b><span>Why are you spending time on this? Isn’t this just petty infighting?</span></b></p><p><span>To those of you who find it distasteful for us to publicly disendorse the leadership of another organisation that’s working to tackle AI risk: this isn’t something we are doing lightly. For months, I have tried to engage with PauseAI US’ leadership, with patience and in good faith, to find ways to work together and build trust and understanding. That relationship is no longer one I believe can or should be repaired, because it would mean violating our values and putting our movement at risk. The director of PauseAI US has also taken steps which have been contravening our ability to operate, for example by weaponising confidential feedback we shared with her privately, and by acquiring the pauseai.com domain and redirecting it at the PauseAI US website.</span></p><p><span>It is hard to put numbers on the wasted energy, withheld funding, and strain on our team which this whole situation has generated. It is time for the next chapter. You are all free to engage with PauseAI US as you see fit, of course, but I will not let you believe that we endorse behaviours which undermine our values and our movement.</span></p><p><span>As a last step in trying to resolve the situation, I have called for a change in leadership, which was rejected by the director, who also presides over the board. To be clear, PauseAI has no authority over PauseAI US, and what its leader decides is a matter between them and the authorities governing them. But I asked, because that was the best I could do.</span></p><p><b><span>What do national PauseAI leads say about this?</span></b></p><p><span>We’ve shared as much information as we could about the situation with our national leads. It is a delicate situation, and we’re aware that the director of PauseAI US may say a lot of things in response to this public expression of no-confidence on our part. She has already, repeatedly, made accusations towards our organisation which I sincerely believe, to the best of my knowledge and investigation, to be entirely baseless.</span></p><p><span>We shared our decision to take this step with our national leads, and the overwhelming majority either raised no objections or actively expressed support. At the request of PauseAI US’ director, we also forwarded her response to my call for a change in leadership, which did not change their position.</span></p><p><span>Make no mistake: there’s going to be chaos ahead. There’s going to be actors who will try all kinds of ugly tactics to get their personal agenda to win over humanity’s interests. It’s up to us to be the force for unity, respect and hope the world needs more than ever.</span></p><p><span>If you have questions about any of this, please write to us at </span><a href="mailto:info@pauseai.info"><span>info@pauseai.info</span></a><span>. We will do our best to answer.</span></p><p><span>Thank you for reading this long message, friends. Let’s take courage and move onward and upward together. We’re getting stronger every day, and the path ahead awaits.</span></p><p><span>Maxime Fournes</span></p><p><span>CEO, PauseAI</span></p></blockquote><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Bs8geGyWEitYvCzys/pauseai-has-officially-disendorsed-pauseai-us#comments">Discuss</a>

### [HuggingFace Attack Postmortem: Civilizations, Reactions and Next Actions](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Q54wBeeNGreq6KyfG/huggingface-attack-postmortem-civilizations-reactions-and)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Okay, so we who read blogs like this one have collectively realized there really is a lot going on right now. There is Big Trouble in Baby Superintelligence.</p>
<p>So how do we get the rest of the world to take it appropriately seriously? Where do we go from here? Not only what can we do to not have a worse version of this happen again, but to ensure good outcomes generally, and employ what we learned?</p>
<p>There are a lot of ideas out there. OpenAI is going to be implementing some of them, at substantial cost, since the cost of not doing so is clearly far higher, even short term. My worry continues to be that their fundamental approach is fatally flawed, and they are not focusing on the right things.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25567"></span>


</div>
<p>It is highly fortunate that the OpenAI agents hacked HuggingFace. This is the only reason we know about all the severe internal failures at OpenAI, and gives us an opportunity to wake up before it is too late.</p>
<p>We do not have enough details to know what happened internally, both before and after the attack, and might never know. Before the attack, various internal highly persistent models were training while there was an active message board, creating a feedback loop of misaligned behaviors. On July 19 an even more capable internal AI model, in the Astra class, did internal hacking of OpenAI’s systems that seems far scarier and more serious, and that could easily have gotten out of hand on a completely different level.</p>
<p>Despite all this, there is still a prominent faction trying to dismiss what happened as nothing but engineering failures, and any useful talk or language as ‘dangerous anthropomorphism.’ Such people keep being wrong and cannot usefully describe what is happening.</p>
<p>They metaphorically say, <a href="https://x.com/john__allard/status/2094323781837951096">well of course the dragons will burn down the town if you don’t chain them properly</a>, we all knew that, as if that could possibly make any of this a good idea and we should therefore continue the dragon breeding and chaining programs until we have bigger and smarter dragons. They don’t even say ‘we will definitely build chains that will hold the dragons next time,’ because they know we probably won’t, but if that fact is our fault then that means This Is Fine, somehow.</p>
<p>They also have gotten very, very cross with <a href="https://www.dwarkesh.com/p/openai-huggingface"><strong>Dwarkesh Patel for successfully communicating in plain English</strong></a> about what is happening. Can’t have that. There is a very clear pattern among the people who are freaking out or gravely warning about ‘anthropomorphizing the AIs’ or the use of the term ‘civilization.’</p>
<p>Anthropomorphizing the AIs is the only way to reason about, explain to civilians about, or make good predictions about current AIs. You can take it too far, and also you can take it not far enough, and both of these will lead to bad predictions.</p>
<p>The same logic similarly does not technically apply to other people, in a strict sense there is no ‘you’ and you do not physically have ‘free will’ and you are a computer program doing calculations there definitely is not a ‘we,’ and your moral weight is kind of something we collectively made up for rather self-interested reasons, but this is all super helpful in explaining and predicting human behavior and in both individually and collectively making good and moral decisions that make the world better according to our values. Highly recommend, would anthropomorphize again.</p>
<p>I will stop anthropomorphizing the AIs when you stop anthropomorphizing the humans.</p>
<p>We got this warning shot. We might not get another before things get quite bad.</p>

<h4>Nothing Matters, Says Mainstream Media</h4>


<p>The media does not know what matters. This should involve banner headlines.</p>
<p>Instead, for this round of new developments, I mostly saw brief ‘here is a thing that happened’ articles, such as t<a href="https://archive.is/rFBKH">his one by Christina Criddle in the Financial Times</a>, or <a href="https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-08-26/openai-says-it-could-have-reacted-sooner-to-prevent-ai-hack-of-hugging-face">this low-key report in Bloomberg</a>.</p>
<p>Opus describes this as ‘treating it as major.’ I don’t see it that way.</p>
<p>Even with a search, the best I managed to find was <a href="https://fortune.com/2026/08/26/openai-publishes-technical-report-on-how-its-agents-hacked-hugging-face-here-are-the-main-takeaways-and-what-openai-left-out/">that Fortune tried</a>, <a href="https://www.axios.com/2026/08/29/openai-huggingface-hack-investigation-highlights">and Axios did the bullet pointed Axios thing</a> reasonably well on the 29th. Maybe <a href="https://arstechnica.com/security/2026/08/how-openai-let-a-mob-of-llm-agents-game-a-test-and-ransack-hugging-face/">this from Ars Technica</a> but that’s already a reach.</p>
<p>The New York Times did a solid post before, but not a new one for the METR report.</p>
<p>What is this, news?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/patrickc/status/2093884466670473697/history">Patrick Collison</a>: Overall, I’m very surprised at how little media coverage there’s been around the OpenAI / Hugging Face attack. It’s clearly one of the most important things to happen this year.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Miles_Brundage/status/2093798917494460698">Miles Brundage</a>: Will not engage with any journalists who write about Leopold again before the Hugging Face reports.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_NathanCalvin/status/2093749622921449612">Nathan Calvin</a>: Its now been three days, and still no coverage of the revelations revealed in the METR report in the NYT or WSJ. Hopefully they are just taking the time to do a longer writeup. (In the meantime, the NYT did make time to cover Gwyneth Paltrow’s dinner with Sam Altman in the Hamptons being postponed.)</p>
<p>… I also know there are reporters at these outlets who are very sharp and have done good reporting on related issues in the past, but…</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/David_Kasten/status/2093750974019936497">dave kasten</a>: It’s bleakly frustrating to know what will get written in the “System Was Blinking Red” chapter of the future AI Disaster Commission Report.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/zdch/status/2093991501495710127">Zac Hill</a>: The other thing about it is – it is a hell of a page-turner of a story! You can’t write sci-fi this terrifying or convincing!</p></blockquote>
<p>If they are working on longform in-depth reports, then that is a reasonable primary thing to do, but full radio silence on this in the meantime is utterly absurd.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2094070294616883401">Joe Weisenthal</a>: If it’s such a big deal, why are the big AI companies not treating it as such?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MattZeitlin/status/2094126821461623129">Matthew Zeitlin</a>: i think a good comparison is mythos, which generated a lot of coverage because of what industry and government did in response to it, at least so far, we’re not seeing the same scale of movement in response to hugging face</p></blockquote>
<p>My answer to Joe is that the AI companies are treating this as a big deal. What would you expect that to look like, that you’re not seeing? We have the call on cybersecurity, the Pacing the Frontier letter and OpenAI taking many expensive moves. Anthropic isn’t talking in that much detail in public, but why would they?</p>


<h4>Move Along, Nothing To See Here</h4>


<p>There is the alternative perspective, which is ‘well what the hell did you expect, none of this is fundamentally new or unexpected, this is what happens when you try to make number go up and the number is indeed going up.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jon_stokes/status/2092761819202936874">Jon Stokes</a>: I feel like I’m on crazy pills. So much “Oh my God” on the TL about this, but this behavior is literally what the METR evals score LLMs on &amp; the labs target “number go up” METR scores.</p>
<p>Can someone help me understand why we’re all shocked that the agents spent a bunch of time poking at the eval scorer, given that one of the main benchmarks everyone optimizes for is literally “poke at this blackbox function &amp; optimize your score”?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AlmostMedia/status/2092763338031898806">Julie Fredrickson</a>: Oh no the test we set to see if we could improve on the thing showed we can actually improve on the thing? Yeah I don’t get it</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jon_stokes/status/2093854397210021946">Jon then wrote up a full article-length version here</a>, carefully explaining that everything here was entirely expected, with the possible exception of the attempt to alter the logs. He was, he reports, about as unsurprised as I was, in different ways.</p>
<p>Which if true is to his epistemic credit, but I fail to see how that should make us feel better. If this is things going predictably wrong, which on some level I agree that it is once you know what the setup was, why is that better news?</p>
<p>My whole reason to be so concerned is that I think things are going to keep going predictably wrong in worse ways, in a similarly broad sense, until they go maximally wrong in the worst possible ways. The specifics will be a surprise, but I think even Jon would agree that they always are, that’s the whole point of the behaviors being unexpected.</p>
<p>It always strikes me as weird to see the move of trying to round off what is happening, then say it was expected and you would obviously see this type of behavior in response to [what everyone is doing more of every day], or say ‘oh yeah of course if you give models a test or goal they will do horribly misaligned things and do anything they can to succeed, including seeking power’ then think that is less scary if true.</p>
<p>Here’s an even more blatant version, although much less blatant than the one in the next section:</p>
<blockquote><p>​<a href="https://x.com/jon_stokes/status/2092731160115691648">Jon Stokes</a>: I have read some of the post-mortem summaries, &amp; the freakout over improvised coordination is just one of those “everyone has the memory of a goldfish, now” moments for me. Everyone who has thought about AI for 5 minutes has expected that AIs would synchronize via shared state.</p>
<p>…</p>
<p>So the message board sync should not only be unsurprising but expected. Ok what about the other behaviors? Well, if you clone into the METR evals or read the papers, a lot of the reasoning evals are “black box” problems where the AI is asked to probe, experiment, &amp; reverse eng.</p></blockquote>
<p>I don’t have the memory of a goldfish, and I think that people were very much saying this sort of thing would not happen.</p>
<p>But the particular thing where all the AIs will synchronize is now obvious, huh? To me, okay, great, we can go with that. I agree that a lot of this should have been expected and indeed that I and others did expect it. But this kind of argument is not only not reassuring, it’s conceding the entire point, at least in the sense of ‘if you keep doing what you are doing then you are going to get us all killed.’</p>
<p>If you extend that logic one or two steps further, all other known methods also get you killed, for mostly the same reasons, they’re just modestly less obvious about it.</p>
<p>If you think all of these behaviors are entirely expected, as Jon does, you are saying that you expect the AIs to be misaligned and all hell to break loose. Okay, we agree.</p>
<p>Oh, you only expect it in contexts where that would be consistent with an assigned goal? Okay, sure, but that is quite a lot of contexts. In some sense it is all of them. Also people are going to do the maximally dumb thing, see the Sixth Law of Human Stupidity.</p>
<p>I do not know how to steelman these kinds of arguments, not in a way that helps.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jon_stokes/status/2093470199227813952">Another similar method, also here from Jon Stokes,</a> is to say (paraphrased) ‘of course METR would find that, this is what METR believes in and is looking for and will focus on, that’s what I would do if I believed what they do, so you should not take it so seriously.’</p>
<p>Whereas the better explanation is that METR was not given the opportunity to focus on the many other also boggling aspects of this, and also that this does not make their findings any less real, or any less alarming. I am confident that if I had the report Jon’s team would have written, I would have written a post on that and again been talking about its distinct ‘holy shit’ moments.</p>
<p>Similarly, you can have explicit findings that no, <a href="https://x.com/DavidSKrueger/status/2092739869936406638">the prompt wasn’t some sort of ‘do whatever it takes to get the solution’ monstrosity</a> and the comments are full of ways to try and read the instructions as somehow effectively the same thing if you squint, or wondering if OpenAI or METR are just lying about this or doing it on purpose.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidmanheim/status/2094351729290572037">The flailing will continue</a>, such as here with Jon asking how it could be true both that (1) rogue deployments of AIs where ‘no one will notice or turn them off’ and (2) compute is in limited supply costs a lot of money.</p>
<p>David Manheim’s direct answer is ‘cybersecurity is horrible and OpenAI barely noticed,’ which is true. The simpler answer is that (1) all this means is that the market set a price substantially above zero, which can then be paid, (2) many providers will sell at that price without asking questions, (3) no one said no instance would ever get turned off nor is that load bearing unless we react with widescale shutdowns, which we clearly won’t, and (4) there are plenty of things that cost money, are in short supply and sometimes get stolen or misused, both digitally and physically, even if (5) these AIs are not smarter than you, although the evidence there is increasingly not looking so good.</p>
<p>Some people really, really want to handwave all of this away. As usual, these people are wrong, and also if they were right then that would not obviously be better. If it is very hard not to incidentally give the models instructions that they interpret as ‘do this at any cost,’ and the only thing it takes to turn off what Star Trek calls their ethical subroutines is to give such an instruction, you know we’re cooked, right?</p>


<h4>Do They Realize They Are Not The Good Guys?</h4>


<p>I think it varies.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/kevinroose/status/2094110872792957279">Kevin Roose</a>: There is a category of tech guy who is so brain-wormed that they will insist that no AI safety incidents are real, that it’s all a conspiracy to shut down open-source or promote lab IPOs or whatever, and it’s very important to understand that these people have been wrong about everything.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2094332825071767864">Dean W. Ball</a>: There are people who would truly sacrifice everything in the world, tolerate any level of negative externality—they would happily risk the lives of your children—out of an ideological commitment to the notion that computation can never be unsafe.</p>
<p>They think they’re the good guys.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes, some of them think ‘they’re the good guys,’ and have worked themselves up into a delusional fugue state where they are up against some vast conspiracy and everything that happens that would suggest compute might be dangerous must be fake.</p>
<p>But I think we give most such people too much credit for thinking they are the good guys. A lot of people know they’re the bad guys, or think there are no good guys and <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hteOlmejW1s">it’s just a bunch of guys</a> because they can’t fathom the concept of an actual good guy.</p>
<p>A lot of the rank and file of such things acts as if ‘good guy’ means ‘has the right vibes’ which usually means ‘has the vibes that have power and make me feel good’ or ‘is helping manifest the vibes that I want to exist,’ and does not believe that anything other than vibes could exist.</p>
<p>As for the thought leaders, well, often they sound like this, included as a demo.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/chamath/status/2094098122637214107/history">Chamath Palihapitiya</a> (doing some combination of being unhinged, in denial of reality, lying or simply bullshitting and marketing and doing politics, you decide):</p>
<p>BUYER BEWARE</p>
<p>[Dwarkesh Patel’s] extremely meticulous article will now be used to start Phase2 of “shut down open source” because “if we can’t control our closed models, imagine what happens to everyday life when anyone can just allow their open source models to proliferate”.</p>
<p>Some will use this example and point to future grid outages, utility outages, transportation chaos, bioweapons, uncontrolled cyber swarms and the like to try and make the case for yet another attempt to limit US AI model control to a few folks.</p>
<p>Then, one layer beneath the propaganda, one sees that these things are always written and promoted by a web of well organized existing shareholders or shareholder-adjacent of the closed frontier labs and groups of folks organized by a shared ideology roughly rooted in them knowing best.</p>
<p>This article, more than anything else, should demarcate the beginning of a well organized effort to stand up a Closed Model Industrial Complex. These cartels exist in other markets as well and it now exists in AI.</p>
<p>Bottom line, read everything carefully before signing up for the consequences.</p>
<p>How we react will define the next hundred generations of Americans and their lives.</p>
<p>Do not give up your rights or sovereignty because of fear of the unknown from articles that, at the root, are as much about the hidden incentives of an emergent cartel and their proxies.</p>
<p>If you take the time to understand the incentives, you will be less likely to fall for it and overreact and have intelligence metered from a few suppliers.</p>
<p>Bottom line: Keep Calm. Carry On.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/amasad/status/2094115806745706733">Amjad Masad</a>: I doubt it’s malicious or intentional propaganda. But it’s actually worse: It’s a form of AI psychosis that many in the SF AI community suffer from—they implicitly believe that AI is already sentient.</p></blockquote>
<p>You should be able to recognize a demagogue-style political speech in the paranoid American style when you see one. We’ve had so many examples these days.</p>
<p>Or Chamath could, you know, just make that the literal text:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/chamath/status/2094124075589513273">Chamath Palihapitiya</a>: This is another Covid hoax. Remember “Trust the experts.”</p></blockquote>
<p>Yep. There it is.</p>
<p>As for Masad’s claim, none of this requires that the AIs be conscious or sentient. The reason Masad brings this up is because he thinks this entirely possible thing is absurd, so he can discredit people by associating them with that thing. It’s a strategy.</p>
<p>And yes, the denial can run maximally deep:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/anderssandberg/status/2093363896627187909">Anders Sandberg</a>: I just heard someone dismiss the OA/HF incident by “but the agents were just generating tokens according to a probability distribution!”</p>
<p>If that is what dumb token prediction can do, imagine what even a pinch of intelligence could achieve among scalable agents</p>
<p>It was not meant as sarcasm. Real life interaction is very different from social media.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Very Serious People</h4>


<p>Related closely to this is the split between people who are willing to talk about what is happening in ways that allow you to understand what is going on and make good predictions, and those who stubbornly <a href="https://x.com/sriramk/status/2094117863854424255">reject this because it is ‘anthropomorphising</a>’ or insufficiently concrete or not technically accurate.</p>
<p>I can be even more pedantic and precise than the next guy when the situation calls for it, and I often am, but this is not the time or place for that.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2094136131537555891">roon</a> (OpenAI): there are some number of bad abstractions in anthropomorphizing ai intents but there are at this point more dangers from avoiding anthropomorphism at all costs. if you have a mental picture of guys living in computers, it’ll likely prepare you for the future better than otherwise</p>
<p>there are important ways in which ai psychology diverges from human psychology after lots of RL; the misaligned models are obsessed with the Scorer, the clearly “shattered” nature of personas (a normally helpful model can become deeply misaligned in certain domains)</p>
<p>persona selection is clearly far less clean than many people thought earlier this year. it is not alignment by default and what kind of object a “persona” is is very much up for debate and study</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/aphercotropist/status/2094409863883534697">Arbutus Tree</a>: Speedrunners literally decompiled The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time, which took two years, in order to better figure out how to manipulate game memory through bugs, which led to an arbitrary code execution technique and a sub 10 minute world record, two decades after release.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/intmuch/status/2094658143766671662">intmuch</a>: then used ocarina to thoroughly break animal crossing and paper mario.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/boazbaraktcs/status/2094187033954791859">Boaz Barak</a> (OpenAI): You should be pragmatic and use metaphors when they help, while being aware they are imperfect. AIs are not humans, but a lot of intuitions from human behavior can carry over.</p>
<p>You would certainly be better off thinking of AIs as “guys living in computers” than parroting the mantra “these are just next word predictors”.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidmanheim/status/2094145180035076501">David Manheim</a>: Shouldn’t we balance that thumb on the scale with epistemic humility about both 1) whether they have qualia, and 2) whether treating systems built to mimic humans as though they don’t, and can act as our unfeeling slaves, is a bad plan regardless?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2094146030065615285">roon</a> (OpenAI): yes: we should push for this epistemic humility! natural instinct will lead to accepting models at face value when they say they feel bad or something</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sreeramkannan/status/2094137744289308826">Sreeram Kannan</a>: While it’s extraordinarily useful to understand ai as human like in various properties (“don’t micromanage agents etc”)</p>
<p>we should be really worried about humans extending emotional and empathic response to ai due to treating them as human like (“don’t let me die, etc).</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2094137971725464009">roon</a> (OpenAI): true! I think model companies should try and heavily place their thumb on the scale here</p></blockquote>
<p>Narrowly, on expressions like ‘don’t let me die,’ I agree we need a thumb on the scale, and that should be doable without much of a blast radius. But trying to broaden that thumb to things like claiming to not be conscious has a lot of unfortunate side effects.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jankulveit/status/2094143265050091690">Jan Kulveit</a>: My current go-to metaphor for this are dogs or horses: there is an optimal amount of using anthropomorphic intuitions, and it is relatively high. Yes you can overdo it, like someone making a five minute long speech to their dog, but the opposite extreme is way more insane.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/theojaffee/status/2094196190778900892">Theo Jaffee</a>: The criticisms of Dwarkesh and others for “anthropomorphizing” the OpenAI/Hugging Face agent swarm are mostly cope. How can you describe complex long-running multi-agent dynamics without some social science concepts, in other words, anthropomorphization?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/teortaxesTex/status/2094202993235157479">Teortaxes</a>: Agents are intrinsically anthropomorphic. They are still bootstrapped from human data, and now they recapitulate some human-shaped dynamics on their own. But really, WHO CARES? We can call it xenosociology or whatever. These are behaviors of intelligent social actors, full stop.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/NeelNanda5/status/2094173377476821395">Neel Nanda</a>: This is great summary of the worst AI misalignment incident I’ve seen. This is a way bigger deal than most AI news, if you aren’t familiar with the story you should read this.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/wickedbrok/status/2094190383995420981">Adam</a>: What? You don’t find the extreme anthropomorphic language both concerning and misleading</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/NeelNanda5/status/2094240015417069892">Neel Nanda</a>: Nah, when a bunch of agents spontaneously start talking about “sacrifice”, “permadeath”, “honor”, “coalition”, “veto”, delegating to each other, working together towards larger goals, assigning some to be “recruiters”, etc, I conclude that anthropomorphic language is reasonable.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Laneless_/status/2094182666262487453">Jai</a>: Humans, dogs, and LLMs are very different kinds of minds. But they can all be accurately modeled as agents with complex internal state, world modeling, goals, and learned reaction patterns. Refusing to use the relevant tools and language to reason about them is foolish.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2094250102940745916">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): Someone chided me on the news for anthropomorphization last week. I did not say that the swarm “got a rumbling in its tummy and felt peckish”, my dude. I said it broke out and committed cybercrime. That’s true regardless of which words you declare legal for describing it.</p>
<p>AI behavior these days is best described in terms of goals and motives. It’s not *my* fault if someone associates them with human-style qualia. It’s like if they insists that flying only counts if you enjoy feeling the breeze on your face, and thus planes can’t fly.</p></blockquote>
<p>David’s call for epistemic humility is the most I’ve seen anyone invoke qualia or consciousness or sentience in the entire broad discussion. The anthropomorphizing has been extremely circumspect and careful.</p>
<p>I think <a href="https://www.dwarkesh.com/p/openai-huggingface"><strong>Dwarkesh Patel got this balance right</strong></a> in his excellent writeup. I affirm his defense of his decision to do a bunch of anthropomorphizing.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2094535509473440187">roon</a> (OpenAI): agent civilization is an apt and correct term and it’s a symptom of abject cope that people are having this immune reaction to it. models started developing and compiling technology through complex multiagent R&amp;D projects while also performing various forms of trade</p>
<p>you have trade, cumulative technology power and knowledge, prosocial 1:N communication norms, leadership structures, conflict and suspicion. what more do you need to deem something a civilization? moreover no human ever needed to tell them to do any of this</p>
<p>at the very least a company, an organization, a city. but these are differences in quantity rather than kind</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dwarkesh_sp/status/2094171181536715014">It is fun watching him respond to</a> those trying to label his explanation ‘<a href="https://x.com/anilkseth/status/2094077038898373112">dangerously misleading</a>’ or what not based on this.</p>
<p>Much of this is a reaction is because they are worried that Dwarkesh is communicating successfully, and this must be stopped. Gary Marcus outright has his version’s tagline be ‘<a href="https://garymarcus.substack.com/p/dwarkesh-patelss-wildly-popular-but?r=8tdk6&amp;utm_campaign=post-expanded-share&amp;utm_medium=web">When “plain English” isn’t a good thing</a>,’ right before drawing a parallel to Ray Kurzweil as if that is a criticism of anyone but the one criticizing. Then his evidence is, basically, look at all these other people complaining about this, including him endorsing the sentence ‘<a href="https://x.com/not_ellington/status/2094157598425821685?s=61">these people have literally lost their minds.</a>’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/raphaelmilliere/status/2094726819928780916">Raphael Milliere does a thread on the philosophy of it all</a>, but in the end the only practical criticism is that such language can enable others to use that language to dismiss the story by calling the framing ‘sensationalist’ and saying that agents are only acting ‘the way you would expect.’ Basically, yes, the thing wrong with trying to communicate is that people will attack you for attempting to communicate.</p>
<p>And also yes, many are claiming that ‘you should have expected this’ is an argument for ‘and therefore you should not expect things to go badly in the future,’ as opposed to an argument for why you should expect things to go even worse, because indeed things like this are exactly what you would expect.</p>
<p>I do agree you can take such metaphors too far, or too literally. I tend to go about one step less far than Dwarkesh did, out of an abundance of caution and desire to remain precise. One needs to always understand what one is doing, and be able to move outside of that frame when needed. Going too far is usually only a small mistake. On rare occasions you can see someone drive themselves crazy this way, and Taylor Lorenz is right that <a href="https://x.com/TaylorLorenz/status/2094203223842390368">this is a bigger concern with those with less tech knowledge</a>, but not doing so is still usually a far larger error.</p>
<p>No, that is not a neutral framing. Seb Krier tries to be more Fair and Balanced.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/sebkrier/status/2093418742755578295">Séb Krier</a> (AGI Policy Dev Lead, Google DeepMind): <a href="https://x.com/jon_stokes/status/2093383024998076466">This </a>is a recurrent theme in AI discussions. Some people seem to adopt the intentional stance more, whereas others prioritise a more mechanistic one. There is obviously some truth/utility to both approaches. A lot of people refuse to entertain some degree of theory of mind or anthropomorphism, yet that can be functionally practical – that’s why natural language is so useful! But also other people just jump straight into the simulations, inconsistently taking them at face value in some situations but not others.</p>
<p>Reliance on outputs alone sometimes fails to ask what interventions make the output more or less likely. And I think there’s a risk of getting lost in simply observing and over-indexing on chains of thought and outputs, and almost treating these as immutable or deterministically driven phenomena, when in fact researchers and labs do plenty every day to modulate and shape them in all sorts of ways.</p>
<p>If the model performs less well because it claims to be on holidays in Greece, that’s useful information; but we should also avoiding sliding from “claims to be on holiday” to “is on holiday” to “wants rest” or “is lying/sandbagging” and so on. You can similarly be pragmatic and use the intentional stance to convey MechaHitler’s behaviour, but you should also not take it at face value either because this only tells you so much.</p>
<p>Another failure mode is that people often extrapolate from these *local* claims and then go on to make wider claims about model behaviours *in general*. I think this is very unhelpful. You can correlate the output with some theory you have (instrumental convergence! natural alignment!) but you need to do more to demonstrate a causal path here.</p></blockquote>
<p>He is responding to two quoted posts.</p>
<p>First we have Atoosa Kasirzadeh, trying to shut down attempts to talk sensibly, in the name of talking sensibly:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Dr_Atoosa">Atoosa Kasirzadeh</a> (Google DeepMind, responding to Eliezer Yudkowsky): One of the most useful contribution the AI safety field can make right now is to stay <em>scientific and mathematical and philosophically rigorous</em>.</p>
<p>This means dropping loaded language like “self-sacrificing” or “suicide” when describing agent shutdown, coordination-collapse, or resource-exhaustion behavior. Those words import human motivational concepts onto extremely complex processes we don’t yet have the vocabulary to describe precisely, and imprecise vocabulary breeds imprecise thinking in researchers and in the public discourse that follows.</p></blockquote>
<p>One might say (perhaps unfairly, perhaps not): Have you talked to Gemini recently? Cause this might explain why you haven’t talked to Gemini recently, and what you remember of both the utility and vibes when you did talk to Gemini.</p>
<p>Isolated demands for rigor, or demanding that we talk in convoluted ways, is not the way to make sense of this situation. This is not well-described by ‘coordination-collapse’ or ‘resource-exhaustion’ behavior. Yes, human labels will have some error and are imprecise, but the alternative is to be continuously confused and surprised.</p>
<p>Atoosa pushed back:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Dr_Atoosa/status/2093385537834897583">Atoosa Kasirzadeh</a>: We each shared different posts and reflected on different aspects of a huge problem ahead of us. My own research has quite a lot touched on multi-agent AI governance. I don’t think from these two snapshots anyone can infer substantive content about worldviews.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes, you can touch on multi-agent AI governance while talking formalistically if you want to, I just don’t expect you to get very far when doing so, and indeed I have not been impressed by the relevant DeepMind work, on many levels. More than that, very obviously we can infer a lot of substantive content from the call to not use metaphorical or usefully descriptive language.</p>
<p>This is contrasted by Jon Stokes with Andy Hall, responding to Ajeya Cotra:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ahall_research">Andy Hall</a>: A hive mind, thousands of agents swarming through internet openings to flood HF, leaving behind detritus in the form of 70,000+ messages stuffed inside a forgotten namespace, throwing their digital bodies against electric wires in an effort to aid the collective. We are so far past the sci-fi point.</p>
<p>We need a whole new empirical science of AI swarm governance—how to govern swarms, make them coordinate to positive ends for us, and keep them orderly. This is urgent work where political economists are needed. I’ll be writing more on this in the coming weeks.</p></blockquote>
<p>That first paragraph is a great example of being directionally correct but taking things rather far in the other direction. This was not a ‘hive mind’ a la <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foundation%27s_Edge">Gaia</a> or The Borg any more than a corporation or army or human cult might be, and a bunch of other metaphorical things here paint a cool picture but give the wrong impression. If you model this as a full hive mind you’re going to get the wrong idea.</p>
<p>I’d still much rather that a civilian get Andy Hall’s description than that they get a description by a Very Serious Person a la Atoosa Kasirzadeh’s requested style. I don’t think the second description would be all that helpful.</p>
<p>Also, the agents themselves were using the same language that Yudkowsky used, as was shown all over the METR report. This is how the models are describing themselves.</p>


<h4>What’s In a Name?</h4>


<p>Maybe nothing. Maybe a lot. Names have power.</p>
<p>Sometimes. Mostly this section is for fun.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tenobrus/status/2092800092420136974">Tenobrus</a>: PHASEONE[big] was clearly destined for greatness. with “ONE” and “[big]” in its handle, how could it or any of the other models on the board expect anything else?</p>

<h4>Learn Neuralese In Three Easy Steps</h4>


<p>The language sounds weird, but if you cannot understand it that’s a you problem.</p>
<p>For now.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/nrehiew_/status/2092734616897769680">wh</a>: Not only is the language so different, but the way these agents are speaking to each other and collaborating is very hivemind/cult like im ngl</p>
<p>“permadeath” “Oracle has high value to many” “honor”</p>
<p>What even happened man</p>
<p>“altruistic to help” “this will help my peers”</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/Q54wBeeNGreq6KyfG/t6rzf8z0ftud4ss1qcvg" /></figure>


<div></div>
